# English Form 5, Form Five English: drafts for review (Lessons 31 lessons (2–32))

Generated by `npm run review:f5-english` from `content/form-5/english/*.json`. Every question and drawing below was built by the engine.
All notes, teach cards, worked examples and paper tasks are **status: draft**. “Authored key” means a person fixed the
right answer (see `AUTHORED.md`); “computed by rule” means code worked it out (a formula, a conversion, a spelling rule).

Teach mode: cards (idea + example + 1 level-1 check) → worked example → practice (levels 1–3) → the note as a summary.

**Summary (the note)**

> A word’s class depends on its use: record can name a document or function as a verb. Adjectives modify nouns; adverbs can modify actions or other expressions, but endings alone do not identify every class. Inflectional spelling also depends on the base: write gives writing, plan gives planned, and study gives studied. A final -e, a consonant before y and a short stressed vowel can matter in different rules. There are exceptions and different patterns: use the relevant model rather than applying a doubling rule to all verbs.

## Lesson 2: Grammar- Revision of parts of speech and some spelling rules

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e5-2 (Reviewing parts of speech and spelling rules)

**Note** (draft)

> A word’s class depends on its use: record can name a document or function as a verb. Adjectives modify nouns; adverbs can modify actions or other expressions, but endings alone do not identify every class. Inflectional spelling also depends on the base: write gives writing, plan gives planned, and study gives studied. A final -e, a consonant before y and a short stressed vowel can matter in different rules. There are exceptions and different patterns: use the relevant model rather than applying a doubling rule to all verbs.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to classify a word by its use and apply a supported inflectional spelling pattern. Builds on: Form Four word classes and computed verb forms. Leads to: study explanations, tense revision and sentence structure.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A word’s class depends on its use: record can name a document or function as a verb. Adjectives modify nouns; adverbs can modify actions or other expressions, but endings alone do not identify every class. Inflectional spelling also depends on the base: write gives writing, plan gives planned, and study gives studied. A final -e, a consonant before y and a short stressed vowel can matter in different rules. There are exceptions and different patterns: use the relevant model rather than applying a doubling rule to all verbs.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original models: The careful member reviews a clear record. Members record their decision carefully. Compare writing, planned and studied. Careful describes a member; carefully describes the manner of reviewing or recording in an appropriate sentence. The grammatical role and the spelling operation need separate explanations.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Read the whole phrase, identify what the word contributes, then state the spelling change required. Check the base and suffix. Do not assume that every word ending in -ly must be an adverb or that every -ed form doubles a consonant.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **word class in use**: a classification based on a word’s function in context
- **adjective**: a modifier of a noun in the model
- **silent-e change**: dropping final silent e before -ing in the studied model
- **consonant doubling**: the spelling change illustrated by the stressed short-vowel model plan


Common mistake: “A spelling can never belong to different word classes.” → “word class in use: a classification based on a word’s function in context.”. The noun and verb uses show why context matters.

## Lesson 3: Speaking-Talk about strategies for studying effectively

Prerequisites: 2 · Skill: e5-3 (Speaking preparation: explaining an effective study plan)

**Note** (draft)

> A useful explanation identifies a particular difficulty and links an action to it. Describing a study plan is different from promising instant success for everyone. An example helps an audience understand the proposed action; acknowledging a constraint makes the plan clearer. This lesson prepares written speaking notes. A teacher or partner should later listen, ask a follow-up question and assess clarity, pronunciation and interaction separately.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to prepare a study explanation with a reason, example and realistic adjustment. Builds on: word functions and earlier study routines. Leads to: listening preparation and purposeful essay planning.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A useful explanation identifies a particular difficulty and links an action to it. Describing a study plan is different from promising instant success for everyone. An example helps an audience understand the proposed action; acknowledging a constraint makes the plan clearer. This lesson prepares written speaking notes. A teacher or partner should later listen, ask a follow-up question and assess clarity, pronunciation and interaction separately.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model extract: I recognise a definition while looking at it but sometimes cannot explain it with the page closed. My plan is to recall an idea, check the attempt and correct a gap. For example, I can explain a word to a partner before comparing my explanation with the lesson.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the audience, purpose and required content. Plan the order, draft in your own words and check each feature against the model. Explain why an edit improves the answer.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **specific difficulty**: the particular problem to address
- **reasoned action**: a proposed step linked to the difficulty
- **illustrating example**: a concrete case clarifying an explanation
- **practical adjustment**: a change recognising a constraint


Common mistake: “A study explanation must conceal the difficulty.” → “specific difficulty: the particular problem to address.”. Naming the problem makes the response relevant.

**Card e5-3-c1** (draft)

## Lesson 4: Grammar- Revision of tenses

Prerequisites: 2, 3 · Skill: e5-4 (Reviewing tense and aspect patterns)

**Note** (draft)

> Simple present can describe a habit or general situation; simple past can present an event as completed in the past. Present progressive uses a present form of be with -ing, while present perfect uses have or has with a past participle. Time reference depends on context, not the form alone. A participle is not automatically a finite verb group. Choose auxiliaries and agreement as well as the lexical form. The exercises name the required pattern so that a different valid meaning is not unfairly marked as the same target.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to form simple, progressive and perfect groups and explain why a complete auxiliary pattern matters. Builds on: word functions, spelling and study language. Leads to: clause structure, sequence of tenses and conditional meanings.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Simple present can describe a habit or general situation; simple past can present an event as completed in the past. Present progressive uses a present form of be with -ing, while present perfect uses have or has with a past participle. Time reference depends on context, not the form alone. A participle is not automatically a finite verb group. Choose auxiliaries and agreement as well as the lexical form. The exercises name the required pattern so that a different valid meaning is not unfairly marked as the same target.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original models: Bih writes the club summary as a regular duty. Yesterday she wrote it. At this moment she is writing it. She has written the current summary. The changing groups present different viewpoints; the form written alone does not supply every complete clause.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Find the subject and the requested pattern, then assemble the full group. Check the distinction between past and participle for irregular verbs. In your own writing, choose the form that fits the intended situation rather than changing tenses without a reason.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **simple present**: the finite pattern used for a habit in the model
- **simple past**: the pattern presenting the model event as completed in the past
- **present progressive**: present be followed by the -ing form
- **present perfect**: have or has followed by a past participle


Common mistake: “A third-person singular habit must always use write without agreement.” → “simple present: the finite pattern used for a habit in the model.”. The studied affirmative pattern needs the singular form.

**Card e5-4-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _simple present: the finite pattern used for a habit in the model._  
> _simple past: the pattern presenting the model event as completed in the past._  
> _present progressive: present be followed by the -ing form._  
> _present perfect: have or has followed by a past participle._  

Check question: `e5-4-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means present be followed by the -ing form?

- ◻️ simple past  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the pattern presenting the model event as completed in the past, rather than present be followed by the -ing form._
- ✅ present progressive
- ◻️ present perfect  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns have or has followed by a past participle, rather than present be followed by the -ing form._
- ◻️ simple present  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the finite pattern used for a habit in the model, rather than present be followed by the -ing form._

Full working after a second miss:

> The auxiliary is is required.  
> So the answer is “present progressive”.

## Lesson 5: Listening- Listen to a passage on organs of an association

Prerequisites: 3, 4 · Skill: e5-5 (Listening preparation: organs of an association)

**Note** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Separate directly stated facts, reasons, proposals and information that remains unknown. An inference needs an identifiable clue and must not turn a fictional case into a universal claim. Notice who speaks and whether a statement is an opinion, a plan or an observed result. This is a written script for listening preparation: no recording is supplied and no hearing or speaking is assessed.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to locate roles and distinguish a proposal from completed action. Builds on: study explanations and tense groups. Leads to: planning from relevant information.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Separate directly stated facts, reasons, proposals and information that remains unknown. An inference needs an identifiable clue and must not turn a fictional case into a universal claim. Notice who speaks and whether a statement is an opinion, a plan or an observed result. This is a written script for listening preparation: no recording is supplied and no hearing or speaking is assessed.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original script: In a fictional reading association, the chair guides discussion, the secretary records decisions and the treasurer reports the association’s funds. Members propose activities and ask questions. The secretary reads back a proposal for a book exchange. Members ask for room availability to be checked before a date is announced. These are the roles in this invented association; its constitution is not supplied. No exchange has yet taken place.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Locate the relevant sentence, state your answer and identify its support. Check whether a question asks what happened, why, or what cannot be concluded. Then use a teacher-provided oral reading for real listening practice if available.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **explicit information**: a detail directly supplied in the passage
- **supported inference**: a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue
- **proposal**: an action suggested rather than necessarily completed
- **limit of evidence**: what this passage cannot establish


Common mistake: “Any plausible invented detail is explicitly stated.” → “explicit information: a detail directly supplied in the passage.”. The detail must actually be present.

**Card e5-5-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _explicit information: a detail directly supplied in the passage._  
> _supported inference: a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue._  
> _proposal: an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._  
> _limit of evidence: what this passage cannot establish._  

Check question: `e5-5-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means an action suggested rather than necessarily completed?

- ◻️ limit of evidence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns what this passage cannot establish, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._
- ◻️ explicit information  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a detail directly supplied in the passage, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._
- ✅ proposal
- ◻️ supported inference  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._

Full working after a second miss:

> Intention and result are different.  
> So the answer is “proposal”.  

**Card e5-5-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _A stated role or action can be located in the text._  
> _A request to verify a source suggests that confirmation is still needed._  
> _Planning a meeting does not show its eventual attendance._  
> _An absent outcome must remain unknown._  

Check question: `e5-5-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Planning a meeting does not show its eventual attendance.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ explicit information

## Lesson 6: Writing- Review of essay types, choosing a topic and planning an essay

Prerequisites: 3, 5 · Skill: e5-6 (Essay types, topic selection and planning)

**Note** (draft)

> Essay labels describe a dominant purpose. Narrative develops events; description creates a focused impression; exposition explains; argument develops a supported position. A response can use several techniques while answering a particular task. Select a manageable topic, identify a controlling idea and order supporting points. An outline is not a collection of unrelated headings. The task and teacher determine required form and length.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to choose an essay approach fitting a task and organise a focused outline. Builds on: study explanations and relevant information. Leads to: developed essays and genre-specific writing.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Essay labels describe a dominant purpose. Narrative develops events; description creates a focused impression; exposition explains; argument develops a supported position. A response can use several techniques while answering a particular task. Select a manageable topic, identify a controlling idea and order supporting points. An outline is not a collection of unrelated headings. The task and teacher determine required form and length.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model extract: Task: Explain how a school club can make notices clearer. Purpose: explanation rather than an invented adventure. Focus: readers need an identifiable activity, required action and source of confirmation.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the audience, purpose and required content. Plan the order, draft in your own words and check each feature against the model. Explain why an edit improves the answer.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **dominant purpose**: the main communicative job requested
- **manageable focus**: a topic narrow enough to develop
- **controlling idea**: the central relationship guiding the outline
- **planned development**: ordered points with relevant support


Common mistake: “Every essay requires an invented adventure.” → “dominant purpose: the main communicative job requested.”. An explaining task needs processes and reasons.

## Lesson 7: Writing- the basics of essay writing

Prerequisites: 6 · Skill: e5-7 (The basics of a developed essay)

**Note** (draft)

> An introduction establishes subject and direction. A body paragraph develops a focused point with explanation or examples and connects it to the overall purpose. Connectors express actual relations such as contrast or cause; inserting however randomly does not create one. A conclusion draws the answer together without introducing an unrelated major claim. No fixed sentence count guarantees quality.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to develop a controlling idea through connected paragraphs and a purposeful conclusion. Builds on: essay purpose and planning. Leads to: reading dialogue and directed writing.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> An introduction establishes subject and direction. A body paragraph develops a focused point with explanation or examples and connects it to the overall purpose. Connectors express actual relations such as contrast or cause; inserting however randomly does not create one. A conclusion draws the answer together without introducing an unrelated major claim. No fixed sentence count guarantees quality.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model extract: A notice helps only when readers can work out what action is expected. A heading may attract attention, but the details must also be clear. Improving a club notice requires a recognisable purpose, useful information and a way to confirm uncertain details.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the audience, purpose and required content. Plan the order, draft in your own words and check each feature against the model. Explain why an edit improves the answer.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **introduction**: an opening establishing topic and direction
- **developed paragraph**: a focused point supported by explanation
- **logical connection**: a link expressing an actual relationship
- **conclusion**: an ending drawing together the developed answer


Common mistake: “An introduction must contain every example.” → “introduction: an opening establishing topic and direction.”. The opening establishes direction; the body develops it.

**Card e5-7-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _introduction: an opening establishing topic and direction._  
> _developed paragraph: a focused point supported by explanation._  
> _logical connection: a link expressing an actual relationship._  
> _conclusion: an ending drawing together the developed answer._  

Check question: `e5-7-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means an opening establishing topic and direction?

- ✅ introduction
- ◻️ developed paragraph  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a focused point supported by explanation, rather than an opening establishing topic and direction._
- ◻️ logical connection  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a link expressing an actual relationship, rather than an opening establishing topic and direction._
- ◻️ conclusion  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an ending drawing together the developed answer, rather than an opening establishing topic and direction._

Full working after a second miss:

> The opening establishes direction; the body develops it.  
> So the answer is “introduction”.  

**Card e5-7-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _A notice needs purpose, information and confirmation._  
> _The heading example explains why a precise title helps._  
> _If a room is unconfirmed, identify the uncertainty._  
> _A useful notice combines a heading with checked details._  

Check question: `e5-7-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> If a room is unconfirmed, identify the uncertainty.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ conclusion  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means an ending drawing together the developed answer. The selected feature instead illustrates a link expressing an actual relationship._
- ◻️ introduction

- ◻️ developed paragraph  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a focused point supported by explanation. The selected feature instead illustrates a link expressing an actual relationship._
- ✅ logical connection

Full working after a second miss:

> A connector must match the relation.  
> So the answer is “logical connection”.  

**Card e5-7-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _An introduction must contain every example. Correction: The opening establishes direction; the body develops it._  
> _Repeating an assertion adds supporting evidence. Correction: Development supplies reasoning or illustration._  
> _Every connector fits every relationship. Correction: A connector must match the relation._  
> _The ending must introduce an unrelated argument. Correction: It resolves the established direction._  

Check question: `e5-7-repair-card` · spot the error, level 1 · **authored key**

> Find the incorrect claim or language form.

- ✅ Every connector fits every relationship.  
  _↳ explanation: A connector must match the relation._
- ◻️ introduction: an opening establishing topic and direction.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: an opening establishing topic and direction. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ developed paragraph: a focused point supported by explanation.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: a focused point supported by explanation. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ logical connection: a link expressing an actual relationship.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: a link expressing an actual relationship. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “Every connector fits every relationship.”.  
> A connector must match the relation.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> A notice needs purpose, information and confirmation. Explain the selected feature.

1. An opening establishing topic and direction.
2. The opening establishes direction; the body develops it.

**Answer:** introduction

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Develop your outline into an essay explaining clearer school messages. Include an introduction, developed body paragraphs and conclusion. Follow the teacher’s required length; the model illustrates development, not a compulsory word count.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> A notice helps only when readers can work out what action is expected. A heading may attract attention, but the details must also be clear. Improving a club notice requires a recognisable purpose, useful information and a way to confirm uncertain details.  
> First, a precise heading identifies the activity. Reading club discussion tells a reader more than Important! The body should explain who is invited and what preparation is needed. Such details connect the heading to an action rather than leaving the audience to guess.  
> A notice also needs honest treatment of uncertainty. If a room is unconfirmed, presenting it as settled may mislead readers. The writer can mark it provisional and identify whom to ask. Clear wording cannot make an uncertain arrangement certain, but it can show what remains to be checked.  
> A useful notice combines a focused heading with checked details. Its value lies in helping readers understand their next step, not in filling a page with impressive words.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] Introduction: an opening establishing topic and direction.
- [ ] Developed paragraph: a focused point supported by explanation.
- [ ] Logical connection: a link expressing an actual relationship.
- [ ] Conclusion: an ending drawing together the developed answer.

## Lesson 8: Reading- read a dialogue on conflict

Prerequisites: 5, 7 · Skill: e5-8 (Reading a dialogue about conflict)

**Note** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Separate directly stated facts, reasons, proposals and information that remains unknown. An inference needs an identifiable clue and must not turn a fictional case into a universal claim. Notice who speaks and whether a statement is an opinion, a plan or an observed result. The passage is original classroom fiction, not a report about a real named organisation.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to identify concerns, supporting reasons and a negotiated proposal. Builds on: essay development and explicit information. Leads to: context clues and directed-writing information.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Separate directly stated facts, reasons, proposals and information that remains unknown. An inference needs an identifiable clue and must not turn a fictional case into a universal claim. Notice who speaks and whether a statement is an opinion, a plan or an observed result. The passage is original classroom fiction, not a report about a real named organisation.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Bih: “Rehearsal covers the quiet reading time we agreed.” Ayuk: “The performance is approaching, so we need practice.” Bih: “I understand the deadline. Could we practise spoken parts outside the reading room, then ask the teacher about another time?” Ayuk: “That could protect the quiet period. I will ask whether a suitable space is available.” Bih: “Let us confirm it before changing the notice.” The dialogue reports a proposal, not a confirmed booking.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Locate the relevant sentence, state your answer and identify its support. Check whether a question asks what happened, why, or what cannot be concluded. Do not add a result or private detail that the passage does not provide.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **explicit information**: a detail directly supplied in the passage
- **supported inference**: a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue
- **proposal**: an action suggested rather than necessarily completed
- **limit of evidence**: what this passage cannot establish


Common mistake: “Any plausible invented detail is explicitly stated.” → “explicit information: a detail directly supplied in the passage.”. The detail must actually be present.

**Card e5-8-c1** (draft)

> _supported inference: a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue._  
> _proposal: an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._  
> _limit of evidence: what this passage cannot establish._

- ◻️ limit of evidence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns what this passage cannot establish, rather than a detail directly supplied in the passage._
- ✅ explicit information

> The detail must actually be present.  
> So the answer is “explicit information”.

## Lesson 9: Vocabulary- contextual clues and collocations

Prerequisites: 2, 8 · Skill: e5-9 (Context clues and collocations)

**Note** (draft)

> A context clue is evidence from surrounding language. A definition, contrast, example or consequence can help explain an unfamiliar word. The clue supports a meaning; it does not justify any guess that sounds interesting. A collocation is a conventional word combination, such as make a decision or heavy rain. A synonym may fit a definition yet sound unnatural with a particular partner. Check grammar and meaning together, and use a dictionary when the context is insufficient.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to infer a word’s meaning from context and choose a natural word partnership. Builds on: Form Four word relationships and reading. Leads to: idiomatic meaning and precise explanations.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A context clue is evidence from surrounding language. A definition, contrast, example or consequence can help explain an unfamiliar word. The clue supports a meaning; it does not justify any guess that sounds interesting. A collocation is a conventional word combination, such as make a decision or heavy rain. A synonym may fit a definition yet sound unnatural with a particular partner. Check grammar and meaning together, and use a dictionary when the context is insufficient.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original example: The path was slippery, so Bih moved carefully to avoid falling. The consequence supports a surface on which slipping is easy. A club can make a decision and do homework, but exchanging make and do mechanically creates unnatural combinations. Heavy rain is ordinary wording; strong rain is not the neutral model taught here.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Locate the exact clue and explain how it supports your interpretation. Separate a contextual inference from a fact not given. When choosing a collocation, consider the whole phrase rather than the isolated meaning of one word.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **definition clue**: surrounding wording that explains the unfamiliar expression
- **contrast clue**: opposed wording that helps reveal a meaning
- **collocation**: a conventional combination of words
- **supported inference**: a conclusion linked to clues actually provided


Common mistake: “A definition clue requires no explanatory wording.” → “definition clue: surrounding wording that explains the unfamiliar expression.”. The nearby explanation supplies the meaning.

**Card e5-9-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _definition clue: surrounding wording that explains the unfamiliar expression._  
> _contrast clue: opposed wording that helps reveal a meaning._  
> _collocation: a conventional combination of words._  
> _supported inference: a conclusion linked to clues actually provided._  

Check question: `e5-9-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ✅ definition clue
- ◻️ collocation  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a conventional combination of words, rather than surrounding wording that explains the unfamiliar expression._
- ◻️ supported inference  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a conclusion linked to clues actually provided, rather than surrounding wording that explains the unfamiliar expression._

Full working after a second miss:

> The nearby explanation supplies the meaning.  
> So the answer is “definition clue”.

## Lesson 10: Introducing Directed writing (forms, elements etc)

Prerequisites: 6, 7, 8, 9 · Skill: e5-10 (Introducing directed writing)

**Note** (draft)

> Directed writing provides a situation and specific instructions. Identify your role, audience, purpose and required form before drafting. Underline every content point and decide where it belongs. A fluent response can still fail its purpose if it omits an essential instruction or uses the wrong format. Use a suitable tone and distinguish information given by the task from assumptions. Check the completed text against the directions rather than marking it by length alone.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to adapt content, audience, purpose and form to a stated writing task. Builds on: essay planning and passage evidence. Leads to: formal speeches, talks, articles and letters.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Directed writing provides a situation and specific instructions. Identify your role, audience, purpose and required form before drafting. Underline every content point and decide where it belongs. A fluent response can still fail its purpose if it omits an essential instruction or uses the wrong format. Use a suitable tone and distinguish information given by the task from assumptions. Check the completed text against the directions rather than marking it by length alone.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: CLASSROOM MODEL NOTICE. Reading Club Meeting. Members are invited to a planning meeting in the school library on Monday afternoon. We will choose a shared reading activity and agree how to care for borrowed books. Please bring a suggestion and a notebook. Issued by the fictional club secretary. The model gives audience, place, time and purpose without claiming an actual school event.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Make a short checklist from the prompt. Plan the order, draft the required form and check each point. Add only suitable invented details where the task permits them. Do not replace a requested notice with an unrelated essay simply because its sentences are correct.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **purpose**: the result the writing is intended to achieve
- **audience**: the intended readers of the response
- **required form**: the kind of text specified in the instructions
- **content checklist**: a record of the points the prompt requires


Common mistake: “A response fulfils every task merely by containing correct spelling.” → “purpose: the result the writing is intended to achieve.”. Correct language must also serve the requested purpose.

**Card e5-10-c1** (draft)

> _audience: the intended readers of the response._  
> _required form: the kind of text specified in the instructions._  
> _content checklist: a record of the points the prompt requires._

## Lesson 11: Grammar- Sentence structure

Prerequisites: 2, 4, 10 · Skill: e5-11 (Sentence structure)

**Note** (draft)

> A simple sentence has one independent clause; it can still have a compound subject or verb. A compound sentence has at least two independent clauses. A complex sentence has an independent clause and at least one dependent clause. A compound-complex sentence combines at least two independent clauses with a dependent clause. Length and the mere presence of and do not determine the type. A comma alone does not normally join independent clauses in formal writing; use an appropriate conjunction or punctuation.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to distinguish independent and dependent clauses and repair clause connections. Builds on: word functions and tense patterns. Leads to: punctuation and connected written arguments.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A simple sentence has one independent clause; it can still have a compound subject or verb. A compound sentence has at least two independent clauses. A complex sentence has an independent clause and at least one dependent clause. A compound-complex sentence combines at least two independent clauses with a dependent clause. Length and the mere presence of and do not determine the type. A comma alone does not normally join independent clauses in formal writing; use an appropriate conjunction or punctuation.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original set: Bih and Ayuk read quietly: simple. Bih reads, and Ayuk writes: compound. Bih reads because the room is quiet: complex. Bih reads because the room is quiet, and Ayuk writes: compound-complex. Count clauses by their structure, not names or verbs in a list.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Mark the boundaries, identify clauses that can stand independently and note dependent markers. Check whether and joins words, phrases or clauses. When repairing a comma splice, preserve the intended relationship rather than inserting any connector at random.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **simple sentence**: a sentence with one independent clause
- **compound sentence**: a sentence with at least two independent clauses and no dependent clause in this classification
- **complex sentence**: a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause
- **compound-complex sentence**: a sentence combining multiple independent clauses with a dependent clause


Common mistake: “Every sentence containing and is compound.” → “simple sentence: a sentence with one independent clause.”. And can join subjects within a single clause.

**Card e5-11-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _simple sentence: a sentence with one independent clause._  
> _compound sentence: a sentence with at least two independent clauses and no dependent clause in this classification._  
> _complex sentence: a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause._  
> _compound-complex sentence: a sentence combining multiple independent clauses with a dependent clause._  

Check question: `e5-11-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a sentence with at least two independent clauses and no dependent clause in this classification?

- ◻️ simple sentence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a sentence with one independent clause, rather than a sentence with at least two independent clauses and no dependent clause in this classification._
- ◻️ compound-complex sentence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a sentence combining multiple independent clauses with a dependent clause, rather than a sentence with at least two independent clauses and no dependent clause in this classification._
- ✅ compound sentence
- ◻️ complex sentence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause, rather than a sentence with at least two independent clauses and no dependent clause in this classification._

Full working after a second miss:

> Both clauses can stand independently.  
> So the answer is “compound sentence”.  

**Card e5-11-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _Bih and Ayuk read quietly._  
> _Bih reads, and Ayuk writes._  
> _Bih reads because the room is quiet._  
> _Bih reads because it is quiet, and Ayuk writes._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a sentence combining multiple independent clauses with a dependent clause. The selected feature instead illustrates a sentence with one independent clause._

- ◻️ complex sentence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a sentence combining an independent clause with a dependent clause. The selected feature instead illustrates a sentence with one independent clause._

Full working after a second miss:

> And can join subjects within a single clause.  
> So the answer is “simple sentence”.

Right-hand side (shuffled): Bih and Ayuk read quietly. · Bih reads because the room is quiet. · Bih reads because it is quiet, and Ayuk writes.
- compound-complex sentence → **Bih reads because it is quiet, and Ayuk writes.**
- complex sentence → **Bih reads because the room is quiet.**
- simple sentence → **Bih and Ayuk read quietly.**

Full working after a second miss:

> The right pairs are:  
> compound-complex sentence → Bih reads because it is quiet, and Ayuk writes.  
> complex sentence → Bih reads because the room is quiet.  
> simple sentence → Bih and Ayuk read quietly.

- ◻️ compound-complex sentence: a sentence combining multiple independent clauses with a dependent clause.

- ✅ A compound sentence must contain only one independent clause.  
  _↳ explanation: Both clauses can stand independently._

## Lesson 12: speech work- English consonant sounds

Prerequisites: 2, 5, 11 · Skill: e5-12 (Speech preparation: contrasting English consonants)

**Note** (draft)

> Consonants can be compared by place, manner and voicing. Lip closure produces the stops /p/ and /b/; /b/ is voiced and /p/ voiceless. The initial sounds /t/ and /d/ are alveolar stops. The pairs /f/ and /v/, and /s/ and /z/, are fricatives: air passes through a narrow opening rather than being fully stopped. Letters and sounds are different systems. This lesson covers selected contrasts, not the full inventory. Written choices do not assess hearing, accent or spoken production.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to identify selected consonant contrasts and explain voicing and manner. Builds on: word forms, listening scripts and clause structure. Leads to: editing written speech and teacher-led pronunciation practice.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Consonants can be compared by place, manner and voicing. Lip closure produces the stops /p/ and /b/; /b/ is voiced and /p/ voiceless. The initial sounds /t/ and /d/ are alveolar stops. The pairs /f/ and /v/, and /s/ and /z/, are fricatives: air passes through a narrow opening rather than being fully stopped. Letters and sounds are different systems. This lesson covers selected contrasts, not the full inventory. Written choices do not assess hearing, accent or spoken production.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original pairs: pin/bin, tie/die, fan/van and sip/zip. Each changes an initial consonant while keeping the rest comparable. /p/ and /b/ use the lips; /f/ and /v/ involve lower lip and upper teeth. A teacher can model these words aloud and check recognition and production.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the requested words, compare place and manner, then identify the voiced member. Use a teacher’s spoken model for actual listening and pronunciation practice. A correct written label does not demonstrate accurate pronunciation.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **voicing contrast**: a difference in vocal-fold vibration in the taught pair
- **bilabial stop**: a consonant made with lip closure in the model
- **alveolar stop**: a stop at the alveolar place in the studied pair
- **fricative**: a consonant with airflow through a narrow opening


Common mistake: “Every different letter marks only a voicing contrast.” → “voicing contrast: a difference in vocal-fold vibration in the taught pair.”. Letters do not automatically specify one articulatory difference.

**Card e5-12-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _voicing contrast: a difference in vocal-fold vibration in the taught pair._  
> _bilabial stop: a consonant made with lip closure in the model._  
> _alveolar stop: a stop at the alveolar place in the studied pair._  
> _fricative: a consonant with airflow through a narrow opening._  

Check question: `e5-12-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ◻️ voicing contrast  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a difference in vocal-fold vibration in the taught pair, rather than a consonant made with lip closure in the model._
- ◻️ alveolar stop  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a stop at the alveolar place in the studied pair, rather than a consonant made with lip closure in the model._
- ✅ bilabial stop
- ◻️ fricative  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a consonant with airflow through a narrow opening, rather than a consonant made with lip closure in the model._

Full working after a second miss:

> That describes the labiodental model, not bilabial closure.  
> So the answer is “bilabial stop”.

> _/f/, /v/, /s/ and /z/ are fricatives._

## Lesson 13: writing- mechanics of writing (punctuation)

Prerequisites: 11, 12 · Skill: e5-13 (Writing mechanics: punctuation and clause boundaries)

**Note** (draft)

> Punctuation guides interpretation rather than replacing sentence structure. A comma alone normally does not join independent clauses in this edited prose. A full stop, semicolon or suitable conjunction can supply a boundary; this exercise specifies the choice. A possessive apostrophe belongs to the owner expression. Paired quotation marks identify direct speech; list commas separate items. Conventions can allow alternatives, such as a final list comma. Follow the teacher’s required quotation and examination style.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to edit boundaries, possession, a list and direct quotation without changing meaning. Builds on: clause structure and prepared speech. Leads to: reading evidence and formal speeches.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Punctuation guides interpretation rather than replacing sentence structure. A comma alone normally does not join independent clauses in this edited prose. A full stop, semicolon or suitable conjunction can supply a boundary; this exercise specifies the choice. A possessive apostrophe belongs to the owner expression. Paired quotation marks identify direct speech; list commas separate items. Conventions can allow alternatives, such as a final list comma. Follow the teacher’s required quotation and examination style.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: The room is open; the notice is ready. Bih’s notebook contains the draft. She wrote, “Bring a pen, a notebook and a folder.” The semicolon joins related complete clauses; the apostrophe identifies the owner; the quoted instruction has a readable list.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Find boundaries and ownership first. Apply the requested convention, reread for meaning and check paired quotation marks. Do not put a comma between an ordinary subject and its verb.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **clause boundary**: punctuation separating complete clauses in the specified pattern
- **singular possession**: an apostrophe pattern marking the singular owner
- **direct quotation**: speech reproduced inside paired quotation marks
- **list separation**: punctuation distinguishing the stated items


Common mistake: “A comma alone is the requested semicolon.” → “clause boundary: punctuation separating complete clauses in the specified pattern.”. Comma and semicolon patterns differ.

**Card e5-13-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _clause boundary: punctuation separating complete clauses in the specified pattern._  
> _singular possession: an apostrophe pattern marking the singular owner._  
> _direct quotation: speech reproduced inside paired quotation marks._  
> _list separation: punctuation distinguishing the stated items._  

Check question: `e5-13-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ◻️ singular possession  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an apostrophe pattern marking the singular owner, rather than speech reproduced inside paired quotation marks._
- ✅ direct quotation
- ◻️ list separation  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns punctuation distinguishing the stated items, rather than speech reproduced inside paired quotation marks._

> A matching closing mark is needed.  
> So the answer is “direct quotation”.

## Lesson 14: Reading- read a passage on combating unemployment

Prerequisites: 9, 13 · Skill: e5-14 (Reading: discussing unemployment)

**Note** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Separate directly stated facts, reasons, proposals and information that remains unknown. An inference needs an identifiable clue and must not turn a fictional case into a universal claim. Notice who speaks and whether a statement is an opinion, a plan or an observed result. The passage is original classroom fiction, not a report about a real named organisation.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to distinguish a proposed response to an employment obstacle from a guaranteed result. Builds on: context clues, sentence structure and editing. Leads to: formal speech and evaluating proposals.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Separate directly stated facts, reasons, proposals and information that remains unknown. An inference needs an identifiable clue and must not turn a fictional case into a universal claim. Notice who speaks and whether a statement is an opinion, a plan or an observed result. The passage is original classroom fiction, not a report about a real named organisation.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original passage: An imaginary youth group finds that some members hear about vacancies after the closing date. It proposes a noticeboard where volunteers copy opportunities and record sources and deadlines. Another member requests help describing existing skills. The group agrees that information and application practice may address particular obstacles but do not create every missing job. The pilot has not begun, so no later employment total is supplied.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Locate the relevant sentence, state your answer and identify its support. Check whether a question asks what happened, why, or what cannot be concluded. Do not add a result or private detail that the passage does not provide.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **explicit information**: a detail directly supplied in the passage
- **supported inference**: a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue
- **proposal**: an action suggested rather than necessarily completed
- **limit of evidence**: what this passage cannot establish


Common mistake: “Any plausible invented detail is explicitly stated.” → “explicit information: a detail directly supplied in the passage.”. The detail must actually be present.

**Card e5-14-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _explicit information: a detail directly supplied in the passage._  
> _supported inference: a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue._  
> _proposal: an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._  
> _limit of evidence: what this passage cannot establish._  

Check question: `e5-14-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue?

- ◻️ limit of evidence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns what this passage cannot establish, rather than a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue._
- ◻️ proposal  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an action suggested rather than necessarily completed, rather than a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue._
- ◻️ explicit information  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a detail directly supplied in the passage, rather than a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue._
- ✅ supported inference

Full working after a second miss:

> The conclusion needs an appropriate clue.  
> So the answer is “supported inference”.

## Lesson 15: Directed writing- speech (formal)

Prerequisites: 10, 14 · Skill: e5-15 (Directed writing: a formal speech)

**Note** (draft)

> A formal speech addresses a stated audience and occasion. Its opening identifies the purpose, its body develops reasons and its ending makes the next step clear. Suitable wording matters more than unnecessary complexity. Preserve a source’s uncertainty: a proposal is not a success already achieved. The original model is not a universal examination template. Live delivery and audience response require separate practice.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to adapt concerns into a formally addressed speech with a supported request. Builds on: directed-writing choices and reading proposals. Leads to: a less formal talk and reported viewpoints.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A formal speech addresses a stated audience and occasion. Its opening identifies the purpose, its body develops reasons and its ending makes the next step clear. Suitable wording matters more than unnecessary complexity. Preserve a source’s uncertainty: a proposal is not a success already achieved. The original model is not a universal examination template. Live delivery and audience response require separate practice.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model extract: Chairperson, teachers and fellow pupils, thank you for the opportunity to speak. I ask the club to consider a noticeboard for checked opportunities. Our fictional source describes members who hear about vacancies after the deadline. That information problem deserves a practical response.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the audience, purpose and required content. Plan the order, draft in your own words and check each feature against the model. Explain why an edit improves the answer.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **formal address**: an opening recognising the stated audience
- **supported purpose**: a request connected to relevant information
- **qualified claim**: a benefit stated without an unsupported guarantee
- **closing request**: a clear next step at the speech’s end


Common mistake: “Every official audience must be addressed as a close friend.” → “formal address: an opening recognising the stated audience.”. The wording should fit this audience.

**Card e5-15-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _formal address: an opening recognising the stated audience._  
> _supported purpose: a request connected to relevant information._  
> _qualified claim: a benefit stated without an unsupported guarantee._  
> _closing request: a clear next step at the speech’s end._  

Check question: `e5-15-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a request connected to relevant information?

- ◻️ formal address  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an opening recognising the stated audience, rather than a request connected to relevant information._
- ◻️ qualified claim  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a benefit stated without an unsupported guarantee, rather than a request connected to relevant information._
- ✅ supported purpose
- ◻️ closing request  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a clear next step at the speech’s end, rather than a request connected to relevant information._

Full working after a second miss:

> The audience needs to understand the purpose.  
> So the answer is “supported purpose”.  

**Card e5-15-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _Chairperson, teachers and fellow pupils, thank you._  
> _Late information supports considering a checked noticeboard._  
> _Information checking does not guarantee selection._  
> _I request permission for a supervised pilot._

> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ formal address  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means an opening recognising the stated audience. The selected feature instead illustrates a benefit stated without an unsupported guarantee._
- ✅ qualified claim
- ◻️ supported purpose  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a request connected to relevant information. The selected feature instead illustrates a benefit stated without an unsupported guarantee._
- ◻️ closing request  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a clear next step at the speech’s end. The selected feature instead illustrates a benefit stated without an unsupported guarantee._

Full working after a second miss:

> Information access and selection differ.  
> So the answer is “qualified claim”.  

**Card e5-15-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>

> _A request needs no reason. Correction: The audience needs to understand the purpose._  
> _A notice proves every applicant will succeed. Correction: Information access and selection differ._  
> _An ending must introduce an unrelated story. Correction: It should resolve the stated purpose._

**Worked example** (draft)

> Chairperson, teachers and fellow pupils, thank you. Explain the selected feature.

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Write a formal speech requesting a supervised noticeboard pilot. Address the school audience, explain the obstacle, develop a reason, acknowledge a limit and end with a request. Rehearse delivery separately.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Chairperson, teachers and fellow pupils, thank you for the opportunity to speak. I ask the club to consider a noticeboard for checked opportunities. Our fictional source describes members who hear about vacancies after the deadline. That information problem deserves a practical response.  
> A useful noticeboard would identify the source and closing date of each notice. Volunteers could check whether old notices should be removed. This would help readers verify information, although it would not guarantee selection or create every opportunity they need.  
> I request permission for a supervised pilot and a review of whether pupils can understand and check the notices. Let us begin with a clear responsibility rather than an unsupported promise. Thank you.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] Formal address: an opening recognising the stated audience.
- [ ] Supported purpose: a request connected to relevant information.
- [ ] Qualified claim: a benefit stated without an unsupported guarantee.
- [ ] Closing request: a clear next step at the speech’s end.
- [ ] Use relevant original details and check sentence boundaries, agreement and spelling.

## Lesson 16: writing- A talk

Prerequisites: 15 · Skill: e5-16 (Writing a talk for a familiar audience)

**Note** (draft)

> A talk for classmates can use direct questions and familiar examples while keeping an organised purpose. An opening question should lead into the topic. Signposting helps listeners follow a sequence they cannot immediately reread. Prepared wording should sound understandable aloud, but the screen cannot assess pace, audibility or interaction. Adapt formality to the task rather than assuming every talk requires a ceremonial address.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to guide classmates through an accessible practical explanation. Builds on: formal speech and audience choices. Leads to: speaking about income proposals.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A talk for classmates can use direct questions and familiar examples while keeping an organised purpose. An opening question should lead into the topic. Signposting helps listeners follow a sequence they cannot immediately reread. Prepared wording should sound understandable aloud, but the screen cannot assess pace, audibility or interaction. Adapt formality to the task rather than assuming every talk requires a ceremonial address.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model extract: Have you read a notice and still wondered what to do next? Today I want to show how we can check a club message before passing it on. Sometimes a small missing detail makes an ordinary message confusing.

> Identify the audience, purpose and required content. Plan the order, draft in your own words and check each feature against the model. Explain why an edit improves the answer.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **audience engagement**: a relevant opening inviting attention to the topic
- **accessible explanation**: wording and examples suited to listeners
- **oral signposting**: phrases guiding listeners through ideas
- **participation invitation**: a relevant action listeners can try


Common mistake: “An opening question must be unrelated.” → “audience engagement: a relevant opening inviting attention to the topic.”. It should focus attention on the topic.

> Have you read a notice and still wondered what to do next? Today I want to show how we can check a club message before passing it on. Sometimes a small missing detail makes an ordinary message confusing.  
> Start by asking what activity the notice names. Next, check who is invited and what action is expected. Finally, check the source of provisional details. For example, a suggested room should not become a confirmed venue simply because we repeat it confidently.  
> Before sharing the next notice, try those questions with a partner. If a detail remains uncertain, ask the responsible person and label the uncertainty. That is a manageable check we can practise together.

## Lesson 17: Speaking- talk about different means to generate income

Prerequisites: 16 · Skill: e5-17 (Speaking preparation: comparing income proposals)

**Note** (draft)

> Explain and question a proposal instead of accepting it because the speaker sounds confident. Separate knowledge, prediction and questions still needing answers. Ask about relevant skills, resources and demand. Cost differs from income, and predicted income does not prove profit. This fictional language exercise supplies no real business recommendation or return. It prepares written discussion notes; turn-taking and delivery require a partner or teacher.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to compare fictional proposals using reasons, questions and cautious conclusions. Builds on: audience-focused talks and qualified claims. Leads to: listening about organising people.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Explain and question a proposal instead of accepting it because the speaker sounds confident. Separate knowledge, prediction and questions still needing answers. Ask about relevant skills, resources and demand. Cost differs from income, and predicted income does not prove profit. This fictional language exercise supplies no real business recommendation or return. It prepares written discussion notes; turn-taking and delivery require a partner or teacher.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model extract: Bih proposes illustrated club bookmarks; Ayuk proposes supervised document-layout help. Bih can show drawings, while Ayuk has practised arranging text. Neither proposal has been tried with interested customers.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the audience, purpose and required content. Plan the order, draft in your own words and check each feature against the model. Explain why an edit improves the answer.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **demonstrated skill**: an ability supported by a relevant example
- **resource question**: a request identifying what an activity requires
- **demand uncertainty**: recognition that customer interest remains untested
- **cautious conclusion**: a judgement matching the available evidence


Common mistake: “Naming an activity proves mastery without evidence.” → “demonstrated skill: an ability supported by a relevant example.”. A demonstration gives relevant support.

**Card e5-17-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>

> _resource question: a request identifying what an activity requires._  
> _demand uncertainty: recognition that customer interest remains untested._  
> _cautious conclusion: a judgement matching the available evidence._

## Lesson 18: Listening- listen to a passage on human resource management

Prerequisites: 17 · Skill: e5-18 (Listening preparation: organising human resources)

**Note** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Separate directly stated facts, reasons, proposals and information that remains unknown. An inference needs an identifiable clue and must not turn a fictional case into a universal claim. Notice who speaks and whether a statement is an opinion, a plan or an observed result. This is a written script for listening preparation: no recording is supplied and no hearing or speaking is assessed.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to follow role allocation and a proposed response to a constraint. Builds on: comparing proposals and relevant questions. Leads to: idioms and narrative viewpoints.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Separate directly stated facts, reasons, proposals and information that remains unknown. An inference needs an identifiable clue and must not turn a fictional case into a universal claim. Notice who speaks and whether a statement is an opinion, a plan or an observed result. This is a written script for listening preparation: no recording is supplied and no hearing or speaking is assessed.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original script: A fictional club plans a reading event. Bih volunteers to welcome visitors, Ayuk to arrange the reading order and Manka to prepare signs. The coordinator asks what support each needs. Manka has a timetable clash, so the group proposes sharing sign preparation and confirming the change with her. The coordinator will review tasks before the event. These are volunteers, not an employment-contract case; the event has not happened.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Locate the relevant sentence, state your answer and identify its support. Check whether a question asks what happened, why, or what cannot be concluded. Then use a teacher-provided oral reading for real listening practice if available.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **explicit information**: a detail directly supplied in the passage
- **supported inference**: a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue
- **proposal**: an action suggested rather than necessarily completed
- **limit of evidence**: what this passage cannot establish


Common mistake: “Any plausible invented detail is explicitly stated.” → “explicit information: a detail directly supplied in the passage.”. The detail must actually be present.

## Lesson 19: Vocabulary- Idiomatic expressions

Prerequisites: 9, 18 · Skill: e5-19 (Idioms in context)

**Note** (draft)

> An idiom has a conventional meaning that may not follow from its individual words. Context helps distinguish figurative use from literal description. Break the ice can mean easing social awkwardness; lend a hand can mean helping. An idiom may fit informal speech without fitting every formal task. Explaining it in plain language is often clearer than substituting another unexplained idiom. Learn the phrase and check its context.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to interpret selected idioms and preserve figurative meaning. Builds on: context clues, collocations and discussion. Leads to: narrative dialogue and word replacement.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> An idiom has a conventional meaning that may not follow from its individual words. Context helps distinguish figurative use from literal description. Break the ice can mean easing social awkwardness; lend a hand can mean helping. An idiom may fit informal speech without fitting every formal task. Explaining it in plain language is often clearer than substituting another unexplained idiom. Learn the phrase and check its context.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Bih says, “I will introduce the new members to break the ice.” Ayuk replies, “I can lend a hand with the chairs.” They check that they are on the same page about the plan, then ask Manka to keep an eye on the time. No frozen water or detached body part is involved.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Locate the phrase, identify the activity and paraphrase in ordinary words. Preserve the speaker’s idea or request. Do not impose a figurative interpretation when a different context describes a literal action.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **break the ice**: help people begin interacting comfortably
- **be on the same page**: share an understanding
- **lend a hand**: help with a task
- **keep an eye on**: watch or monitor something


Common mistake: “The host physically shattered a frozen lake.” → “break the ice: help people begin interacting comfortably.”. The social context means easing initial awkwardness.

## Lesson 20: Writing- Narrative essay

Prerequisites: 7, 19 · Skill: e5-20 (Writing a narrative essay)

**Note** (draft)

> A narrative develops events through a viewpoint. A complication motivates action; choices and consequences make the sequence more than a timetable. Select details contributing to action or meaning. Keep a clear time frame, with tense changes justified by relationships. Dialogue should reveal a concern or advance the event. A resolution shows change without necessarily solving every future problem. The original model is not prescribed literature.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to develop a consistent viewpoint and causal events leading to resolution. Builds on: essay organisation, tense choices and dialogue. Leads to: reported viewpoints and tense sequence.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A narrative develops events through a viewpoint. A complication motivates action; choices and consequences make the sequence more than a timetable. Select details contributing to action or meaning. Keep a clear time frame, with tense changes justified by relationships. Dialogue should reveal a concern or advance the event. A resolution shows change without necessarily solving every future problem. The original model is not prescribed literature.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model extract: I reached the reading room carrying the notice I had copied that morning. Visitors were gathering, but the door was locked. I looked at my handwriting and feared I had ruined the event.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the audience, purpose and required content. Plan the order, draft in your own words and check each feature against the model. Explain why an edit improves the answer.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **narrative viewpoint**: the perspective presenting events
- **complication**: a problem motivating consequential action
- **causal development**: events linked through choices and consequences
- **resolution and reflection**: an ending showing change and significance


Common mistake: “A first-person narrator automatically knows every private thought.” → “narrative viewpoint: the perspective presenting events.”. The viewpoint gives the narrator’s experience, not all minds.

**Card e5-20-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _narrative viewpoint: the perspective presenting events._  
> _complication: a problem motivating consequential action._  
> _causal development: events linked through choices and consequences._  
> _resolution and reflection: an ending showing change and significance._  

Check question: `e5-20-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a problem motivating consequential action?

- ◻️ narrative viewpoint  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns the perspective presenting events, rather than a problem motivating consequential action._
- ◻️ resolution and reflection  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an ending showing change and significance, rather than a problem motivating consequential action._
- ◻️ causal development  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns events linked through choices and consequences, rather than a problem motivating consequential action._
- ✅ complication

Full working after a second miss:

> The complication motivates relevant action.  
> So the answer is “complication”.  

**Card e5-20-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _I reached the room and feared I had ruined the event._  
> _Visitors gather while the room is locked._  
> _The narrator admits the mistake and seeks help._  
> _The notice is corrected and the narrator reflects._  

Check question: `e5-20-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> I reached the room and feared I had ruined the event.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ complication  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a problem motivating consequential action. The selected feature instead illustrates the perspective presenting events._
- ◻️ resolution and reflection  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means an ending showing change and significance. The selected feature instead illustrates the perspective presenting events._
- ◻️ causal development  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means events linked through choices and consequences. The selected feature instead illustrates the perspective presenting events._
- ✅ narrative viewpoint

Full working after a second miss:

> The viewpoint gives the narrator’s experience, not all minds.  
> So the answer is “narrative viewpoint”.  

**Card e5-20-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _A first-person narrator automatically knows every private thought. Correction: The viewpoint gives the narrator’s experience, not all minds._  
> _The problem must be unrelated to later choices. Correction: The complication motivates relevant action._  
> _Disconnected times alone develop a meaningful narrative. Correction: Choices and consequences link events._  
> _An ending must erase every consequence. Correction: Change can coexist with remaining consequences._  

Check question: `e5-20-repair-card` · spot the error, level 1 · **authored key**

> Find the incorrect claim or language form.

- ◻️ complication: a problem motivating consequential action.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: a problem motivating consequential action. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ resolution and reflection: an ending showing change and significance.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: an ending showing change and significance. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ causal development: events linked through choices and consequences.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: events linked through choices and consequences. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ✅ An ending must erase every consequence.  
  _↳ explanation: Change can coexist with remaining consequences._

> Change can coexist with remaining consequences.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> I reached the room and feared I had ruined the event. Explain the selected feature.

1. The perspective presenting events.
2. The viewpoint gives the narrator’s experience, not all minds.

**Answer:** narrative viewpoint

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Write a narrative where an unconfirmed detail causes difficulty. Maintain viewpoint, develop a choice and consequence, and end with resolution or reflection. Include dialogue only where relevant.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> I reached the reading room carrying the notice I had copied that morning. Visitors were gathering, but the door was locked. I looked at my handwriting and feared I had ruined the event.  
> “Who confirmed this room?” Manka asked. I started to say everyone had mentioned it. Then I remembered the coordinator’s words: the room was still a suggestion. I had turned a possibility into an instruction. Instead of blaming the caretaker, I admitted the mistake and asked Manka to help find the teacher.  
> The teacher arranged an available supervised space while we explained the change. The beginning was delayed, and some visitors had to leave. We could not undo that inconvenience. Afterwards I corrected the notice and marked the new arrangement clearly.  
> When I folded the old sheet away, it no longer looked like a small copying mistake. Repeating a detail confidently was different from checking it.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] Narrative viewpoint: the perspective presenting events.
- [ ] Complication: a problem motivating consequential action.
- [ ] Causal development: events linked through choices and consequences.
- [ ] Resolution and reflection: an ending showing change and significance.
- [ ] Use relevant original details and check sentence boundaries, agreement and spelling.

## Lesson 21: Grammar- Sequence of tenses

Prerequisites: 4, 20 · Skill: e5-21 (Sequence of tenses and reported viewpoints)

**Note** (draft)

> Under a past reporting frame, backshift often changes present to past and present perfect to past perfect; will may become would. Pronouns and words such as tomorrow can also change when the reporting viewpoint changes. A form should preserve who did what and when. Backshift is not compulsory in every report: information still true or relevant can remain in a present form. Past perfect normally stays past perfect. Exercises must state the intended frame where several choices could be valid.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to preserve references and relative time when backshift is requested while recognising contexts that retain a tense. Builds on: tense patterns and narrative time. Leads to: conditional meanings and accurate reporting in articles.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Under a past reporting frame, backshift often changes present to past and present perfect to past perfect; will may become would. Pronouns and words such as tomorrow can also change when the reporting viewpoint changes. A form should preserve who did what and when. Backshift is not compulsory in every report: information still true or relevant can remain in a present form. Past perfect normally stays past perfect. Exercises must state the intended frame where several choices could be valid.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: Bih said, I have written the note. A later report with backshift says she had written the note. Ayuk said he would return the next day when reporting is from a later viewpoint. Compare an ongoing fact reported as still true: the club coordinator says or said that the room is available, if availability remains current in the stated context.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Mark the reporting time and the original reference first. Adjust tense, pronouns and relative time only as the intended perspective requires. Check auxiliaries and participles separately; a correct reporting pattern cannot rescue an incorrect verb form.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **backshift**: a tense adjustment relative to a past reporting frame
- **reference preservation**: keeping the same people and things identified after reporting
- **relative time**: time wording interpreted from a viewpoint
- **unchanged past perfect**: retaining an already past-perfect form in the taught report


Common mistake: “Every present form must change in every reported statement.” → “backshift: a tense adjustment relative to a past reporting frame.”. Still-true information can sometimes retain its tense.

**Card e5-21-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _backshift: a tense adjustment relative to a past reporting frame._  
> _reference preservation: keeping the same people and things identified after reporting._  
> _relative time: time wording interpreted from a viewpoint._  
> _unchanged past perfect: retaining an already past-perfect form in the taught report._
