- ◻️ relative time  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns time wording interpreted from a viewpoint, rather than keeping the same people and things identified after reporting._
- ◻️ unchanged past perfect  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns retaining an already past-perfect form in the taught report, rather than keeping the same people and things identified after reporting._
- ✅ reference preservation

> The referents must remain clear and consistent.  
> So the answer is “reference preservation”.

## Lesson 22: Conditional sentences

Prerequisites: 4, 21 · Skill: e5-22 (Conditional sentences)

**Note** (draft)

> A conditional links a condition and a result. Common zero-condition models use present forms for general patterns. A first-condition model uses if + present and will + base for a possible future. A second-condition model commonly uses if + past and would + base for an imagined present or future. A third-condition model uses if + past perfect and would have + participle for an unreal past. These are useful patterns, not a claim that every possible conditional must fit one rigid template.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to select and justify a conditional model for a general, possible, imagined or unreal-past relationship. Builds on: tense revision and reported viewpoints. Leads to: accurate qualifications in explanatory writing.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A conditional links a condition and a result. Common zero-condition models use present forms for general patterns. A first-condition model uses if + present and will + base for a possible future. A second-condition model commonly uses if + past and would + base for an imagined present or future. A third-condition model uses if + past perfect and would have + participle for an unreal past. These are useful patterns, not a claim that every possible conditional must fit one rigid template.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original models: If the room is available, the club meets there as a general rule. If it is available tomorrow, we will meet there. If we had another room, we would share the work. If we had booked earlier, we would have obtained the room. The last situation is imagined about the past; it does not report that the earlier booking actually happened.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Choose the intended time and degree of reality before the form. In the ordinary first-condition model, do not insert will into the if-clause simply because the time is future. Other modal uses exist, so a special context can justify another construction. Clause order can change without reversing the condition and result.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **zero-condition model**: a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship
- **first-condition model**: a common if-present/will-base pattern for a possible future
- **second-condition model**: an if-past/would-base pattern for an imagined present or future
- **third-condition model**: an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past


Common mistake: “Every conditional necessarily concerns one future event.” → “zero-condition model: a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship.”. A zero-condition model can describe a general pattern.

**Card e5-22-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _zero-condition model: a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship._  
> _first-condition model: a common if-present/will-base pattern for a possible future._  
> _second-condition model: an if-past/would-base pattern for an imagined present or future._  
> _third-condition model: an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past._  

Check question: `e5-22-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past?

- ◻️ second-condition model  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an if-past/would-base pattern for an imagined present or future, rather than an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past._
- ◻️ first-condition model  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a common if-present/will-base pattern for a possible future, rather than an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past._
- ✅ third-condition model
- ◻️ zero-condition model  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship, rather than an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past._

Full working after a second miss:

> The model imagines a past alternative, rather than asserting its occurrence.  
> So the answer is “third-condition model”.  

**Card e5-22-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _If the room is available, the club meets there under its general rule._  
> _If the room is free tomorrow, we will meet there._  
> _If we had another room now, we would use it._  
> _If we had booked earlier, we would have obtained the room._  

Check question: `e5-22-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> If we had booked earlier, we would have obtained the room.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ zero-condition model  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a condition/result pattern describing a general relationship. The selected feature instead illustrates an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past._
- ◻️ first-condition model  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a common if-present/will-base pattern for a possible future. The selected feature instead illustrates an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past._
- ✅ third-condition model
- ◻️ second-condition model  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means an if-past/would-base pattern for an imagined present or future. The selected feature instead illustrates an if-past-perfect/would-have-participle pattern for an unreal past._

Full working after a second miss:

> The model imagines a past alternative, rather than asserting its occurrence.  
> So the answer is “third-condition model”.  

**Card e5-22-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _Every conditional necessarily concerns one future event. Correction: A zero-condition model can describe a general pattern._  
> _The ordinary first-condition model always requires will in both clauses. Correction: Use present in the if-clause and will plus base in the result here._  
> _The past form here proves the imagined room existed yesterday. Correction: The past form can mark hypothetical distance rather than past time._  
> _The third-condition model reports that the earlier condition actually happened. Correction: The model imagines a past alternative, rather than asserting its occurrence._

## Lesson 23: Vocabulary- vocabulary related to e- commerce

Prerequisites: 9, 22 · Skill: e5-23 (Vocabulary for e-commerce)

**Note** (draft)

> E-commerce is trade through electronic systems. Its vocabulary distinguishes stages: a listing describes an offer, an order requests goods, a payment record identifies payment and delivery supplies the item to its destination. An enquiry is not necessarily an order. A receipt does not alone establish delivery or quality. These fictional examples supply no real platform, price, transaction or consumer-law statement.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to distinguish listing, order, payment record and delivery. Builds on: context clues and conditional language. Leads to: quantity expressions and factual articles.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> E-commerce is trade through electronic systems. Its vocabulary distinguishes stages: a listing describes an offer, an order requests goods, a payment record identifies payment and delivery supplies the item to its destination. An enquiry is not necessarily an order. A receipt does not alone establish delivery or quality. These fictional examples supply no real platform, price, transaction or consumer-law statement.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: A seller posts a folder listing. A customer asks about size, then orders after details are agreed. The seller issues a payment record when payment is received. A later delivery note records arrival at the stated destination. The model distinguishes evidence without claiming every transaction follows this procedure.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the action or document before choosing the term. Explain what the record supports and what it does not prove. Do not report an offer as a completed exchange.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **product listing**: a description of an item offered
- **order**: a request for specified goods
- **payment record**: a record identifying a payment
- **delivery**: supply of an item to its destination


Common mistake: “A listing proves delivery already happened.” → “product listing: a description of an item offered.”. Offer and delivery are distinct.

**Card e5-23-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _product listing: a description of an item offered._  
> _order: a request for specified goods._  
> _payment record: a record identifying a payment._  
> _delivery: supply of an item to its destination._  

Check question: `e5-23-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a record identifying a payment?

- ◻️ order  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a request for specified goods, rather than a record identifying a payment._
- ◻️ delivery  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns supply of an item to its destination, rather than a record identifying a payment._
- ◻️ product listing  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a description of an item offered, rather than a record identifying a payment._
- ✅ payment record

Full working after a second miss:

> Payment and delivery evidence differ.  
> So the answer is “payment record”.  

**Card e5-23-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _The page describes the offered folder._  
> _The customer requests the selected folder._  
> _The receipt identifies payment for the order._  
> _The folder reaches the agreed destination._

- ◻️ payment record  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a record identifying a payment. The selected feature instead illustrates a request for specified goods._

Full working after a second miss:

> Asking for information and ordering differ.

## Lesson 24: Grammar- Quantifiers and qualifiers

Prerequisites: 2, 9, 22, 23 · Skill: e5-24 (Quantifiers and qualifiers)

**Note** (draft)

> Quantifiers express quantity: several proposals, much information, a little time. Countability is a property of the noun’s use, so a dictionary and the sentence matter. A few suggests some countable items, while few often emphasises scarcity; a little and little make a similar contrast with an uncountable amount. Qualifiers such as fairly or very can modify the degree of an adjective. They do not automatically provide a numerical measurement. A statement with some or fairly should not be rewritten as all or completely without evidence.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to match quantity expressions to noun uses and explain how a qualifier changes the degree of a description. Builds on: word classes, context and conditional statements. Leads to: resource explanations and appropriately limited claims.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Quantifiers express quantity: several proposals, much information, a little time. Countability is a property of the noun’s use, so a dictionary and the sentence matter. A few suggests some countable items, while few often emphasises scarcity; a little and little make a similar contrast with an uncountable amount. Qualifiers such as fairly or very can modify the degree of an adjective. They do not automatically provide a numerical measurement. A statement with some or fairly should not be rewritten as all or completely without evidence.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original models: Several members read the fairly clear proposal. A few offered questions, so the number was small but positive. The group needed a little more information. Information is uncountable in this use; an item of information introduces a countable unit. Fairly modifies clear, not the exact attendance.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the noun use, then choose a compatible quantifier. For a qualifier, name the word it modifies and the strength of the claim. Preserve small positive quantity, absence and totality as different meanings.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **count quantifier**: an expression compatible here with a plural countable noun
- **amount quantifier**: an expression for an uncountable amount in the model
- **positive small quantity**: the some meaning of a few or a little in context
- **degree qualifier**: a modifier changing the strength of a description


Common mistake: “Much proposals is the required model.” → “count quantifier: an expression compatible here with a plural countable noun.”. Much does not fit this plural countable use.

**Card e5-24-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _count quantifier: an expression compatible here with a plural countable noun._  
> _amount quantifier: an expression for an uncountable amount in the model._  
> _positive small quantity: the some meaning of a few or a little in context._  
> _degree qualifier: a modifier changing the strength of a description._  

Check question: `e5-24-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means the some meaning of a few or a little in context?

- ◻️ amount quantifier  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an expression for an uncountable amount in the model, rather than the some meaning of a few or a little in context._
- ◻️ degree qualifier  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a modifier changing the strength of a description, rather than the some meaning of a few or a little in context._
- ◻️ count quantifier  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an expression compatible here with a plural countable noun, rather than the some meaning of a few or a little in context._
- ✅ positive small quantity

Full working after a second miss:

> The phrase indicates a small positive number.  
> So the answer is “positive small quantity”.  

**Card e5-24-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _Several proposals gives a countable quantity._  
> _A little information gives an amount without counting items._  
> _A few attended means some attended._  
> _Fairly qualifies clear in the proposal description._  

Check question: `e5-24-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Several proposals gives a countable quantity.
> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ amount quantifier  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means an expression for an uncountable amount in the model. The selected feature instead illustrates an expression compatible here with a plural countable noun._
- ◻️ positive small quantity  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means the some meaning of a few or a little in context. The selected feature instead illustrates an expression compatible here with a plural countable noun._
- ✅ count quantifier
- ◻️ degree qualifier  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a modifier changing the strength of a description. The selected feature instead illustrates an expression compatible here with a plural countable noun._

Full working after a second miss:

> Much does not fit this plural countable use.  
> So the answer is “count quantifier”.  

**Card e5-24-c3** (draft)

> A correction must preserve the intended meaning and form.
>
> _Much proposals is the required model. Correction: Much does not fit this plural countable use._  
> _Many information is the ordinary pattern in this use. Correction: Use much or an appropriate amount phrase rather than many._  
> _A few always means no one. Correction: The phrase indicates a small positive number._  
> _Fairly supplies an exact numerical percentage by itself. Correction: A degree word does not automatically supply a measurement._  

Check question: `e5-24-repair-card` · spot the error, level 1 · **authored key**

> Find the incorrect claim or language form.

- ◻️ positive small quantity: the some meaning of a few or a little in context.  
  _↳ explanation: This is correct: the some meaning of a few or a little in context. Look for a claim that conflicts with a rule or distinction taught in Learn._
- ◻️ degree qualifier: a modifier changing the strength of a description.

## Lesson 25: Reading- read a passage on natural resources

Prerequisites: 22, 24 · Skill: e5-25 (Reading: natural resources and proposed uses)

**Note** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Separate directly stated facts, reasons, proposals and information that remains unknown. An inference needs an identifiable clue and must not turn a fictional case into a universal claim. Notice who speaks and whether a statement is an opinion, a plan or an observed result. The passage is original classroom fiction, not a report about a real named organisation.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to compare proposed resource uses and identify limits of evidence. Builds on: quantity, conditions and supported reading. Leads to: newspaper reporting and environmental discussion.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Separate directly stated facts, reasons, proposals and information that remains unknown. An inference needs an identifiable clue and must not turn a fictional case into a universal claim. Notice who speaks and whether a statement is an opinion, a plan or an observed result. The passage is original classroom fiction, not a report about a real named organisation.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original passage: A fictional village group discusses a shared stream. Some members propose watering a school garden; others want to keep water clear for existing users. The group proposes seeking appropriate technical guidance before changing how water is taken. It records both concerns and plans a discussion with affected users. No measured flow, laboratory result or permission to alter the stream is supplied. Compatibility of the uses in every season remains unestablished.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Locate the relevant sentence, state your answer and identify its support. Check whether a question asks what happened, why, or what cannot be concluded. Do not add a result or private detail that the passage does not provide.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **explicit information**: a detail directly supplied in the passage
- **supported inference**: a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue
- **proposal**: an action suggested rather than necessarily completed
- **limit of evidence**: what this passage cannot establish


Common mistake: “Any plausible invented detail is explicitly stated.” → “explicit information: a detail directly supplied in the passage.”. The detail must actually be present.

**Card e5-25-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _explicit information: a detail directly supplied in the passage._  
> _supported inference: a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue._  
> _proposal: an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._  
> _limit of evidence: what this passage cannot establish._  

Check question: `e5-25-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue?

- ◻️ proposal  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an action suggested rather than necessarily completed, rather than a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue._
- ◻️ explicit information  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a detail directly supplied in the passage, rather than a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue._
- ◻️ limit of evidence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns what this passage cannot establish, rather than a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue._
- ✅ supported inference

Full working after a second miss:

> The conclusion needs an appropriate clue.  
> So the answer is “supported inference”.

> _A stated role or action can be located in the text._  
> _A request to verify a source suggests that confirmation is still needed._  
> _Planning a meeting does not show its eventual attendance._  
> _An absent outcome must remain unknown._

## Lesson 26: Directed writing- Newspaper article

Prerequisites: 10, 21, 25 · Skill: e5-26 (Directed writing: a newspaper article)

**Note** (draft)

> An article helps readers identify what happened and why it matters. A headline identifies focus; a lead presents the central event; later paragraphs develop relevant details and attribute their source. Quote only words supplied in the task and preserve meaning in indirect reports. Do not invent a real witness’s words or present a proposal as completed. This model reports classroom fiction, not current news or a universal examination format.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to separate reported facts, attribution and unconfirmed outcomes in an article. Builds on: directed writing, reported viewpoints and resource reading. Leads to: expository explanation and accurate reporting.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> An article helps readers identify what happened and why it matters. A headline identifies focus; a lead presents the central event; later paragraphs develop relevant details and attribute their source. Quote only words supplied in the task and preserve meaning in indirect reports. Do not invent a real witness’s words or present a proposal as completed. This model reports classroom fiction, not current news or a universal examination format.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model extract: Village group seeks guidance on shared stream

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the audience, purpose and required content. Plan the order, draft in your own words and check each feature against the model. Explain why an edit improves the answer.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **focused headline**: a heading identifying the central event
- **informative lead**: an opening supplying the main event
- **attribution**: identification of the information’s source
- **status accuracy**: preservation of whether action is planned or completed


Common mistake: “A headline must claim success when only a plan exists.” → “focused headline: a heading identifying the central event.”. The heading should match the event’s status.

**Card e5-26-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _focused headline: a heading identifying the central event._  
> _informative lead: an opening supplying the main event._  
> _attribution: identification of the information’s source._  
> _status accuracy: preservation of whether action is planned or completed._  

Check question: `e5-26-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means a heading identifying the central event?

- ◻️ informative lead  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an opening supplying the main event, rather than a heading identifying the central event._
- ◻️ attribution  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns identification of the information’s source, rather than a heading identifying the central event._
- ◻️ status accuracy  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns preservation of whether action is planned or completed, rather than a heading identifying the central event._
- ✅ focused headline

Full working after a second miss:

> The heading should match the event’s status.  
> So the answer is “focused headline”.  

**Card e5-26-c2** (draft)

>
> _Village group seeks guidance on shared stream._  
> _The group proposes guidance before changing water use._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a heading identifying the central event. The selected feature instead illustrates an opening supplying the main event._

> So the answer is “informative lead”.  

**Card e5-26-c3** (draft)

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Report the fictional stream discussion in a newspaper-style article. Include headline, lead, attributed details and unknowns. Use supplied facts; invent no real witnesses or direct quotations.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Village group seeks guidance on shared stream  
> A fictional village group proposes seeking technical guidance before changing stream use for a school garden. Members recorded concerns about watering the garden and keeping water clear for existing users.  
> According to the classroom passage, discussion with affected users is planned. No measured flow or laboratory result is supplied. There is also no permission to alter the stream, so the discussion cannot be reported as an authorised construction project.  
> Compatibility of the uses in every season remains unestablished. Further information is part of the proposal, rather than an outcome achieved already.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] Focused headline: a heading identifying the central event.
- [ ] Informative lead: an opening supplying the main event.
- [ ] Attribution: identification of the information’s source.
- [ ] Status accuracy: preservation of whether action is planned or completed.
- [ ] Use relevant original details and check sentence boundaries, agreement and spelling.

**Practice** `e5-26-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

- ✅ attribution
- ◻️ informative lead  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an opening supplying the main event, rather than identification of the information’s source._
- ◻️ focused headline  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a heading identifying the central event, rather than identification of the information’s source._

## Lesson 27: Writing- Expository essay

Prerequisites: 7, 26 · Skill: e5-27 (Writing an expository essay)

**Note** (draft)

> Expository writing explains an idea, relationship or process. A process explanation connects stages and shows why they matter; it is more than a list of commands. Examples clarify abstract points, and transitions signal actual sequence or cause. Keep the focus on explanation even if the conclusion mentions an application. Define necessary terms and qualify claims whose evidence is limited.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to explain a process through connected stages and examples. Builds on: developed essays and attributed reporting. Leads to: reasoned environmental discussion.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Expository writing explains an idea, relationship or process. A process explanation connects stages and shows why they matter; it is more than a list of commands. Examples clarify abstract points, and transitions signal actual sequence or cause. Keep the focus on explanation even if the conclusion mentions an application. Define necessary terms and qualify claims whose evidence is limited.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model extract: Checking a notice means examining whether its details are supported before using or repeating them. The process begins with the message, moves to its sources and ends by identifying what still needs confirmation.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the audience, purpose and required content. Plan the order, draft in your own words and check each feature against the model. Explain why an edit improves the answer.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **expository focus**: the explanatory purpose guiding the text
- **connected stages**: steps related in a meaningful process
- **clarifying example**: a case explaining an abstract point
- **limited conclusion**: an ending preserving the explanation’s scope


Common mistake: “Every explanation requires an adventure plot.” → “expository focus: the explanatory purpose guiding the text.”. The text develops understanding rather than requiring a plot.

>

> _clarifying example: a case explaining an abstract point._  
> _limited conclusion: an ending preserving the explanation’s scope._

**Worked example** (draft)

**Answer:** expository focus

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Write an expository essay explaining how to check a classroom message. Define the process, develop stages with examples and conclude with a realistic account of what it achieves.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Checking a notice means examining whether its details are supported before using or repeating them. The process begins with the message, moves to its sources and ends by identifying what still needs confirmation.  
> First, identify the activity and expected action. An invitation should distinguish attending a discussion from bringing materials or preparing a contribution. This prevents a familiar heading from hiding an unclear instruction.  
> Next, check the source of details affecting action. A suggested room differs from a room confirmed by the responsible person. Comparing the notice with its source preserves the information’s status instead of turning a possibility into a fact.  
> Finally, make unresolved details visible. Label a venue provisional and identify whom to ask. Checking cannot prevent every later change; it helps readers understand the evidence available when a message is shared.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] Expository focus: the explanatory purpose guiding the text.
- [ ] Connected stages: steps related in a meaningful process.
- [ ] Clarifying example: a case explaining an abstract point.
- [ ] Limited conclusion: an ending preserving the explanation’s scope.
- [ ] Use relevant original details and check sentence boundaries, agreement and spelling.

## Lesson 28: Speaking- talk about ways of protecting the environment

Prerequisites: 25, 27 · Skill: e5-28 (Discussing environmental protection)

**Note** (draft)

> An environmental discussion should connect a specific action to its purpose. Reducing waste prevents some waste at source; reusing gives an appropriate object another use; recycling requires a suitable treatment route. Sorting alone does not prove a material is recycled. Use the school’s actual instructions for practical work and keep unknown or dangerous waste outside pupils’ handling tasks. This lesson practises language through fictional proposals rather than arranging an unsupervised activity.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to support a proposal with a reason and a practical limit in a prepared discussion. Builds on: reading natural-resource proposals and writing explanations. Leads to: air-pollution listening preparation and careful conclusions.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> An environmental discussion should connect a specific action to its purpose. Reducing waste prevents some waste at source; reusing gives an appropriate object another use; recycling requires a suitable treatment route. Sorting alone does not prove a material is recycled. Use the school’s actual instructions for practical work and keep unknown or dangerous waste outside pupils’ handling tasks. This lesson practises language through fictional proposals rather than arranging an unsupervised activity.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original dialogue: Bih proposes avoiding unnecessary printing. Ayuk suggests reusing a clean box for materials. Another member asks which materials the local collection arrangement accepts. The group will check the teacher’s instructions before acting. Each proposal explains a possible benefit without claiming that one small action solves every environmental problem.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Prepare short turns: state the point, give a relevant reason and invite a response. Use fictional people if the subject is private. Practise with a partner when available; written choices here do not establish oral fluency or listening ability. Challenge an idea without insulting the speaker.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **reduction**: avoiding part of the resource use or waste at source
- **reuse**: giving an appropriate object another use
- **recycling**: transforming a material through a suitable treatment route
- **qualified proposal**: a suggested action recognising relevant conditions or limits


Common mistake: “Reduction necessarily means producing more waste.” → “reduction: avoiding part of the resource use or waste at source.”. The aim is to prevent part of the use or waste.

**Card e5-28-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _reduction: avoiding part of the resource use or waste at source._  
> _reuse: giving an appropriate object another use._  
> _recycling: transforming a material through a suitable treatment route._  
> _qualified proposal: a suggested action recognising relevant conditions or limits._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a suggested action recognising relevant conditions or limits, rather than giving an appropriate object another use._
- ◻️ recycling  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns transforming a material through a suitable treatment route, rather than giving an appropriate object another use._
- ✅ reuse

## Lesson 29: Vocabulary- prefixes and suffixes, synonyms and antonyms

Prerequisites: 2, 9, 19, 28 · Skill: e5-29 (Prefixes, suffixes, synonyms and antonyms)

**Note** (draft)

> A prefix attaches before a base, while a suffix attaches after it. Either can affect meaning, and suffixes can change word class. Unhappy uses a negative prefix; helpful uses a suffix to form an adjective. Not every visible letter sequence is a productive affix. Synonyms have related meanings in context, while antonyms oppose meanings on a relevant scale. A word may have several senses, so neither a prefix nor a dictionary synonym permits every possible substitution.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to explain word formation and preserve contextual meaning when replacing a word. Builds on: context clues and idioms. Leads to: accurate editing of environmental and formal writing.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> A prefix attaches before a base, while a suffix attaches after it. Either can affect meaning, and suffixes can change word class. Unhappy uses a negative prefix; helpful uses a suffix to form an adjective. Not every visible letter sequence is a productive affix. Synonyms have related meanings in context, while antonyms oppose meanings on a relevant scale. A word may have several senses, so neither a prefix nor a dictionary synonym permits every possible substitution.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model: help, helpful and unhelpful belong to a word family. A calm room may be described as peaceful; an open door contrasts with a closed door. Replacing peaceful with an unrelated look-alike word would not preserve the description. A change of suffix must still fit the required grammatical position.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Find the base, identify the added part and check the resulting word in its sentence. For synonyms and antonyms, preserve the relevant sense and grammatical construction. Verify unfamiliar forms rather than adding un- or -ness to every word mechanically.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **prefix**: an element added before a base
- **suffix**: an element added after a base
- **synonym in context**: a word with a related meaning in the stated use
- **antonym in context**: a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect


Common mistake: “A prefix always follows its base.” → “prefix: an element added before a base.”. Its position is before the base.

**Card e5-29-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _prefix: an element added before a base._  
> _suffix: an element added after a base._  
> _synonym in context: a word with a related meaning in the stated use._  
> _antonym in context: a word expressing an opposed meaning on the relevant aspect._

- ◻️ suffix  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns an element added after a base, rather than an element added before a base._
- ◻️ synonym in context  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a word with a related meaning in the stated use, rather than an element added before a base._

Full working after a second miss:

> Its position is before the base.  
> So the answer is “prefix”.

## Lesson 30: Listening- listen to a passage on air pollution

Prerequisites: 28, 29 · Skill: e5-30 (Listening preparation: air-pollution information)

**Note** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Separate directly stated facts, reasons, proposals and information that remains unknown. An inference needs an identifiable clue and must not turn a fictional case into a universal claim. Notice who speaks and whether a statement is an opinion, a plan or an observed result. This is a written script for listening preparation: no recording is supplied and no hearing or speaking is assessed.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to extract sources and distinguish observation, proposal and measured result. Builds on: environmental discussion and contextual vocabulary. Leads to: concord and careful formal writing.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Read the whole passage before answering. Separate directly stated facts, reasons, proposals and information that remains unknown. An inference needs an identifiable clue and must not turn a fictional case into a universal claim. Notice who speaks and whether a statement is an opinion, a plan or an observed result. This is a written script for listening preparation: no recording is supplied and no hearing or speaking is assessed.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original script: In a fictional club discussion, a teacher explains that air pollution can come from combustion sources such as motor vehicles, as well as dust. A pupil proposes recording where visible smoke or dust is noticed, with adult guidance and without approaching sources. The teacher says these observations do not measure pollutant concentrations or establish anyone’s diagnosis. The group proposes asking a suitable specialist about available information. No survey result or medical conclusion is supplied.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Locate the relevant sentence, state your answer and identify its support. Check whether a question asks what happened, why, or what cannot be concluded. Then use a teacher-provided oral reading for real listening practice if available.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **explicit information**: a detail directly supplied in the passage
- **supported inference**: a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue
- **proposal**: an action suggested rather than necessarily completed
- **limit of evidence**: what this passage cannot establish


Common mistake: “Any plausible invented detail is explicitly stated.” → “explicit information: a detail directly supplied in the passage.”. The detail must actually be present.

**Card e5-30-c1** (draft)

> Distinguish the ideas used in this lesson.
>
> _explicit information: a detail directly supplied in the passage._  
> _supported inference: a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue._  
> _proposal: an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._  
> _limit of evidence: what this passage cannot establish._  

Check question: `e5-30-terms` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which term means an action suggested rather than necessarily completed?

- ◻️ explicit information  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a detail directly supplied in the passage, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._
- ✅ proposal
- ◻️ supported inference  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._
- ◻️ limit of evidence  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns what this passage cannot establish, rather than an action suggested rather than necessarily completed._

Full working after a second miss:

> Intention and result are different.  
> So the answer is “proposal”.  

**Card e5-30-c2** (draft)

> Read the whole example before identifying its function.
>
> _A stated role or action can be located in the text._  
> _A request to verify a source suggests that confirmation is still needed._  
> _Planning a meeting does not show its eventual attendance._  
> _An absent outcome must remain unknown._  

Check question: `e5-30-cases` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which description best fits the selected feature?

- ◻️ supported inference  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a conclusion grounded in a relevant textual clue. The selected feature instead illustrates what this passage cannot establish._
- ◻️ proposal  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means an action suggested rather than necessarily completed. The selected feature instead illustrates what this passage cannot establish._
- ✅ limit of evidence
- ◻️ explicit information  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That means a detail directly supplied in the passage. The selected feature instead illustrates what this passage cannot establish._

Full working after a second miss:

> The evidence does not carry that universal scope.  
> So the answer is “limit of evidence”.

## Lesson 31: Grammar - Concord

Prerequisites: 4, 11, 24, 30 · Skill: e5-31 (Concord: agreement with the grammatical subject)

**Note** (draft)

> The finite verb agrees with the grammatical subject in the taught present patterns. In the chair of the groups, chair is the singular nucleus; groups is inside a complement. Each of the reporters takes singular agreement in the model. Distinct subjects Bih and Ayuk joined by and take plural agreement. A nearby plural noun does not automatically control the verb. Collective nouns and some coordinated constructions vary according to meaning and convention; the exercises use explicit cases rather than treating every collective expression alike.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to choose a present verb agreeing with its subject despite an intervening complement or a coordinated subject. Builds on: sentence structure, quantifiers and the revised verb forms. Leads to: editing formal and informal letters.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> The finite verb agrees with the grammatical subject in the taught present patterns. In the chair of the groups, chair is the singular nucleus; groups is inside a complement. Each of the reporters takes singular agreement in the model. Distinct subjects Bih and Ayuk joined by and take plural agreement. A nearby plural noun does not automatically control the verb. Collective nouns and some coordinated constructions vary according to meaning and convention; the exercises use explicit cases rather than treating every collective expression alike.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original models: The chair of the groups writes the summary. The members of the club write it. Each of the reporters checks a detail. Bih and Ayuk prepare the notice. The complements do not replace the subject, and a participle alone does not provide a missing finite verb.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Mark the whole subject, find its nucleus or coordination, then choose the present form. Check that an apparent correction has not changed tense. Read the full clause rather than matching the last noun to the nearest verb.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **subject nucleus**: the central word controlling agreement in the model group
- **singular each**: the singular agreement pattern with each in the model
- **coordinated plural**: agreement with distinct subjects joined by and
- **finite agreement**: the matching form within a complete finite clause


Common mistake: “Groups must control the verb simply because it is nearer.” → “subject nucleus: the central word controlling agreement in the model group.”. The nearer noun is inside a complement.

> _finite agreement: the matching form within a complete finite clause._

- ◻️ finite agreement

## Lesson 32: Writing- Formal and informal letter

Prerequisites: 10, 13, 26, 31 · Skill: e5-32 (Directed writing: formal and informal letters)

**Note** (draft)

> Letters adapt content, layout and register to recipient and purpose. A formal request to an unnamed official can pair Dear Sir or Madam with Yours faithfully in the British convention modelled here. A letter to a friend can use a personal greeting and friendly closing. Both require clear paragraphs and accurate information. Formality does not require inflated vocabulary; friendliness does not remove sentence boundaries. The teacher should confirm local address, date, length and closing requirements.

**Learn: Your goal and connections** (draft)

> By the end, you should be able to adapt core information to a formal request and a personal letter. Builds on: audience, punctuation, directed writing and concord. Leads to: independent writing with controlled register and clear connections.

**Learn: Understand the pattern** (draft)

> Letters adapt content, layout and register to recipient and purpose. A formal request to an unnamed official can pair Dear Sir or Madam with Yours faithfully in the British convention modelled here. A letter to a friend can use a personal greeting and friendly closing. Both require clear paragraphs and accurate information. Formality does not require inflated vocabulary; friendliness does not remove sentence boundaries. The teacher should confirm local address, date, length and closing requirements.

**Learn: Read the original model** (draft)

> Original model extract: Formal model: Reading Club, Fictional School, Bamenda. Date: the day of drafting. Dear Sir or Madam, I write for our club to request permission for a supervised discussion on checking school notices. We would like pupils to practise distinguishing confirmed details from suggestions.

**Learn: Apply and check** (draft)

> Identify the audience, purpose and required content. Plan the order, draft in your own words and check each feature against the model. Explain why an edit improves the answer.

**Learn: key words and examples** (draft)

- **formal purpose and register**: a clear request suited to the official recipient
- **matched greeting and closing**: a consistent pair following the stated convention
- **informal personal tone**: friendly wording suited to the named correspondent
- **preserved core information**: facts kept consistent despite changed audience


Common mistake: “A formal letter must hide its purpose in long words.” → “formal purpose and register: a clear request suited to the official recipient.”. The request should be direct and understandable.

**Card e5-32-c1** (draft)

>

> _matched greeting and closing: a consistent pair following the stated convention._  
> _informal personal tone: friendly wording suited to the named correspondent._  
> _preserved core information: facts kept consistent despite changed audience._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns a clear request suited to the official recipient, rather than facts kept consistent despite changed audience._
- ◻️ informal personal tone  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That concerns friendly wording suited to the named correspondent, rather than facts kept consistent despite changed audience._

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Write a formal request to an unnamed school official for the notice-checking discussion, then an informal letter telling a friend about it. Preserve unconfirmed arrangements in both. Follow the teacher’s required layout and length.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Formal model: Reading Club, Fictional School, Bamenda. Date: the day of drafting. Dear Sir or Madam, I write for our club to request permission for a supervised discussion on checking school notices. We would like pupils to practise distinguishing confirmed details from suggestions.  
> The session would use original classroom examples and partner work. A teacher would need to confirm the venue and timing before invitations are issued. Please let us know whether the proposal can be considered and whom we should consult. Yours faithfully, Bih, Club secretary.  
> Informal model: Fictional School, Bamenda. Date: the day of drafting. Dear Amina, Our reading club is planning a discussion about checking notices. I thought you would enjoy helping with examples. We want to show how a suggested room can accidentally become a confirmed venue in a copied message.  
> The arrangements are still provisional, so please do not make travel plans from this letter. I will write when the teacher confirms the time and place. If you have a confusing fictional notice we could discuss, I would like to hear your idea. Best wishes, Bih.

- [ ] Matched greeting and closing: a consistent pair following the stated convention.
- [ ] Informal personal tone: friendly wording suited to the named correspondent.
- [ ] Preserved core information: facts kept consistent despite changed audience.
- [ ] Use relevant original details and check sentence boundaries, agreement and spelling.
